<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
Let $M$ be a smooth manifold and $k,l \in \mathbb N \cup \{0,+\infty\}$ with $k + l \geq 2$. A \emph{Lorentz--Finsler structure of regularity $C^{(k,l)}$} is a map $L: TM \to \mathbb R$ such that the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} For each fiberwise linear chart $(x^i,v^i)$ on $TM \setminus \mathbf 0$, $\partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma L$ exists and is continuous for all multi-indices $\beta,\sigma$ satisfying $|\beta| \leq k$ and $|\sigma| \leq k + l - |\beta|$;
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} $L(cv) = c^2 L(v)$ for all $c > 0$ and $v \in TM$;
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} For all $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf 0$, the vertical Hessian $g_{ij}(v):=\partial_{v^i} \partial_{v^j} L$ has Lorentzian signature $(-,+,\dots,+)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
We say that a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime $(M,L)$ satisfies the \emph{distributional strong energy condition} if $\mathrm{Ric} \geq 0$ on $\Omega^T$ in the distributional sense, i.e., $\langle \mathrm{Ric}, \mu\rangle \geq 0$ for any nonnegative volume density $\mu$ on $TM$ compactly supported in $\Omega^T$.
Similarly, we say that $\mathrm{Ric} \geq K$ \emph{timelike distributionally} for $K \in \mathbb R$ if the distribution $\mathrm{Ric} + 2 K L$ is nonnegative.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,3)}$ Finsler spacetime satisfying the distributional strong energy condition, and let $\mathcal{K} \subseteq \Omega^T$ be compact.
    Then for any $\delta >0$, there exists $\varepsilon_0 =\varepsilon_0(\mathcal{K},\delta) >0$ such that for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0)$ and $v \in \mathcal{K}$ with $2 \widecheck{L}_\varepsilon(v)=-1$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon](v) \ge - \delta.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a globally hyperbolic $C^{(1,3)}$
Finsler spacetime satisfying
$\operatorname{Ric}\geq K$ timelike distributionally
for some $K\in\mathbb R$.
Suppose that $\Sigma\subseteq M$ is a $C^2$ spacelike
Cauchy hypersurface that is $\beta$-contracting,
where $\beta>0$ and additionally
$\beta>\sqrt{(n-1)|K|}$ if $K<0$. Then
\begin{equation*}
    \sup_M d_\Sigma\leq T_{K,\beta},
\end{equation*}
where 
\begin{equation*}
    T_{K,\beta}:=
    \begin{cases}
        \dfrac{n-1}{\beta},
        & K=0,\\[1.2ex]
        \sqrt{\dfrac{n-1}{K}}\,
        \operatorname{arctan}\!
        \left(\dfrac{\sqrt{(n-1)K}}{\beta}\right),
        & K>0,\\[1.6ex]
        \sqrt{\dfrac{n-1}{|K|}}\,
        \operatorname{arctanh}\!
        \left(\dfrac{\sqrt{(n-1)|K|}}{\beta}\right),
        & K<0.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
In particular, $(M,L)$ is future timelike geodesically
incomplete.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,3)}$ Finsler spacetime satisfying the distributional strong energy condition, $\Sigma \subseteq M$ a $C^2$ compact spacelike hypersurface that is contracting. 
Then $(M,L)$ is future timelike geodesically incomplete.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a globally hyperbolic $C^{(1,3)}$ Finsler spacetime which satisfies the distributional null energy condition and contains a $C^2$ trapped surface $S$ and a noncompact Cauchy hypersurface. 
Then there exists a future null geodesic $\eta$ emanating orthogonally from $S$ that is future incomplete.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a globally hyperbolic $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime, equipped with a $C^1$ measure $\mathfrak m$, satisfying 
\[
\mathrm{Ric}^N_\mathfrak m \geq K\exp\biggl( \frac{4(\kappa-1)}{n-1}\Psi \biggr)
\]
timelike distributionally for some $K\in\mathbb R$, $N \in (-\infty,1] \cup [n,\infty]$ and $\kappa$ timelike admissible for $N$.
Suppose that $\Sigma\subseteq M$ is a $C^2$ spacelike Cauchy hypersurface with $\theta_\kappa <-\beta$, where $\beta>0$ and, additionally, $\beta>\sqrt{|K|/c_{N,\kappa}}$ when $K<0$.
Then we have
\[
\sup_\eta \lim_{t \uparrow T} \varphi_\eta(t) \leq T^{(N,\kappa)}_{K,\beta},
\]
where $\eta:[0,T) \to M$ runs over all future unit speed timelike geodesics maximizing the distance from $\Sigma$ and we set 
\[
    T^{(N,\kappa)}_{K,\beta}
    :=
    \begin{cases}
        \dfrac{1}{c_{N,\kappa}\beta},
        & K=0,\\[1.2ex]
        \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{c_{N,\kappa}K}}\,
        \arctan\!\left(
            \dfrac{1}{\beta}\sqrt{\dfrac{K}{c_{N,\kappa}}}
        \right),
        & K>0,\\[1.6ex]
        \sqrt{\dfrac{1}{c_{N,\kappa}|K|}}\,
        \operatorname{arctanh}\!\left(
            \dfrac{1}{\beta}\sqrt{\dfrac{|K|}{c_{N,\kappa}}}
        \right),
        & K<0.
    \end{cases}
\]
In particular, every such $\eta$ is future $\kappa$-incomplete and, when $N \in [n,\infty)$ and $\kappa=1$, we have $\sup_M d_\Sigma \le T^{(N,1)}_{K,\beta}$ and $(M,L)$ is future timelike geodesically incomplete.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
Let $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime equipped with a $C^1$ measure $\mathfrak m$ satisfying $\mathrm{Ric}^N_{\mathfrak m}\geq0$ timelike distributionally for some $N\in(-\infty,1]\cup[n,\infty]$.
Suppose that $\Sigma\subseteq M$ is a compact $C^2$ spacelike hypersurface such that $\theta_\kappa<0$ for some $\kappa$ which is timelike admissible for $N$.

Then there exists a future inextendible unit speed timelike geodesic $\eta:[0,T)\to M$, starting orthogonally to $\Sigma$, that is future $\kappa$-incomplete.
In particular, when $N\in[n,\infty)$ and $\kappa=1$, this geodesic is future incomplete in the usual sense.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
Let $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ be a globally hyperbolic $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime of dimension $n\geq3$, equipped with a $C^1$ measure  $\mathfrak m$, satisfying the distributional null $N$-energy condition for some $N\in(-\infty,2]\cup[n,\infty]$.
Suppose that $M$ contains a noncompact Cauchy hypersurface and a compact orientable $C^2$ spacelike submanifold $S$ of codimension $2$ satisfying $\theta_\kappa^\pm<0$ for some $\kappa$ which is null admissible for $N$. 

Then there exists a future inextendible null geodesic $\eta:[0,T)\to M$, starting orthogonally to $S$, which is future $\kappa$-incomplete.
In particular, if $N\in[n,\infty)$ and $\kappa=1$, $\eta$ is future incomplete in the usual sense.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
Let $M, N$ be smooth manifolds and let $k,l \in \mathbb{N}_0 \cup \{+\infty\}$, $\alpha \in [0,1]$, and $\mathcal{U} \subseteq TM$ be an open set.
    A map $f: \mathcal{U} \to N$ is said to be $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$-\emph{regular} if, in any bundle chart $(x^i,v^i)$ on $\mathcal{U}$, for its coordinate representation $f(x,v)$ the partial derivatives $\partial_x^{\beta} \partial_v^{\sigma} f$ exist and are
    locally $\alpha$-H\"older continuous 
    for all multi-indices $\beta,\sigma$ with $|\beta|\leq k$, $|\sigma| \leq k + l - |\beta|$.
    We write $C^{(k,l),\alpha}(\mathcal{U})$ for the space of $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$-regular functions on $\mathcal{U} \subseteq TM$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
The fact that no mixed derivatives appear in the expression for $\partial / \partial v^i$ is a consequence of the vector bundle structure of $TM$.
Thus, more generally, it makes sense to consider maps from an open set $\mathcal{U} \subseteq \mathcal{V}$ of an arbitrary smooth vector bundle $\mathcal{V}$ over $M$ to any other smooth manifold $N$ that are of class $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{Definition: Lorentz Finsler structure}
Let $M$ be a smooth manifold, $k,l \in \mathbb{N}_0 \cup \{+\infty\}$ with $k+l \geq 2$, and $\alpha \in [0,1]$. A function $L: TM \to \mathbb R$ is called a $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ \emph{Lorentz--Finsler structure} on $M$ if
\begin{enumerate}
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}[(1)] $L \in C^{(k,l),\alpha}(TM \setminus \mathbf0)$, where $\mathbf{0} \subseteq TM$ denotes the zero section;
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}[(2)] $L(cv) = c^2 L(v)$ for all $v \in TM$ and $c>0$;
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}[(3)] For any $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf 0$, the symmetric $(n \times n)$-matrix
    \begin{equation*}
        g_{ij}(v) := \frac{\partial^2 L}{\partial v^i \partial v^j}(v)
    \end{equation*}
    is non-degenerate with signature $(-,+,\ldots,+)$.
\end{enumerate}
We call the pair $(M,L)$ a $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ \emph{Lorentz--Finsler manifold}.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
Let $g$ be a $C^{k,\alpha}$ Lorentzian metric on $M$ for $k \in \mathbb N_0 \cup \{+\infty\}$ and $\alpha \in [0,1]$. Then it naturally induces a $C^{(k,\infty), \alpha}$ Lorentz--Finsler structure on $M$ via
\begin{equation*}
    L(v):= \frac{1}{2}g(v,v).
\end{equation*}
As is easily seen,
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{\partial^2 L}{\partial v^i \partial v^j}(v) = g\biggl( \frac{\partial}{\partial x^i}, \frac{\partial}{\partial x^j} \biggr)
\end{equation*}
for all $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$, so in this case, the induced Lorentzian metric $g_v$ as in \eqref{eq:g_v} is equal to $g$, and thus independent of the direction $v$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{ex:Randers}
An example of a low regularity \emph{Riemann--Finsler structure} (whose definition is exactly the same as that of a Lorentz--Finsler structure $L$, except that $g_{ij}(v)$ is required to be positive definite for all $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$) is given by \emph{Randers structures} of the form
\begin{equation*}
    L(v):=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sqrt{g(v,v)} + \beta(v)\bigr)^2,
\end{equation*}
where $g$ is a $C^{k,\alpha}$ Riemannian metric and $\beta$ is a $C^{k,\alpha}$ $1$-form such that $|\beta|_g < 1$. 
The resulting Randers metric $L$ is then of regularity $C^{(k,\infty),\alpha}$. 
Lorentzian Randers structures defined on all of $TM$ are difficult to describe; we refer to the discussion in \cite{Minguzzi-LightconesFinsler}.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
An explicit Lorentz--Finsler structure of regularity $C^{(1,3)}$, the class for which we establish our unweighted singularity theorems, can be constructed as follows. 
Let $M = \mathbb R \times \mathbb R^n$, $a(t):=1 + |t|^{3/2}$ and define $L: TM = (\mathbb R \times \mathbb R^n) \times (\mathbb R \times \mathbb R^n) \to \mathbb R$ by
\begin{equation*}
    2L\bigl((t,x),(\tau,v)\bigr):= - 5\tau^2 + a(t)^2 \sum_{i= 1}^n (v^i)^2 + \biggl( |\tau|^{9/2} + a(t)^{9/2} \sum_{i=1}^n |v^i|^{9/2} \biggr)^{4/9}.
\end{equation*}
An explicit computation shows that $L$ is of regularity $C^{(1,3)}$, but not of regularity $C^{(1,4)}$, and that its vertical Hessian has Lorentzian signature at every $(\tau,v) \neq 0$.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ Lorentz--Finsler manifold. 
If there is a continuous timelike vector field $X$ on $M$, we call $(M,L)$ a $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ \emph{Finsler spacetime} that is \emph{time-oriented} by $X$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{Lemma: lowregFinsler cone structure}
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,3)}$ Finsler spacetime. 
Then the future causal cone distribution $\Omega^C  \subseteq TM$ is a locally Lipschitz proper cone structure in the sense of \textnormal{\cite{Minguzziconestructure}}.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
This was already shown in \cite[Thm.\ 2.52]{Minguzziconestructure}, noting that the $C^2$ assumption in its statement is not needed in the proof.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,3),\alpha}$ Finsler spacetime.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{def:FormalChristoffels}
    \gamma^i_{jk}(v):= \frac{1}{2} g^{il}(v) \left( \frac{\partial g_{lk}}{\partial x^j} + \frac{\partial g_{jl}}{\partial x^k} - \frac{\partial g_{jk}}{\partial x^l}\right)(v)
\end{equation}
for $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$,
where $(g^{ij}(v))$ denotes the inverse matrix of $(g_{ij}(v))$.
Further, set for $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$
\begin{equation*}
    G^i(v):= \gamma^i_{jk}(v) v^j v^k, \qquad
    N^i_j(v):=\frac{1}{2} \frac{\partial G^i}{\partial v^j}(v).
\end{equation*}
By convention, we put $G^i(0) := 0$ and $N^i_j(0):=0$. 
The coefficients of the \emph{Chern connection} of $(M,L)$ are now defined for $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$ via
\begin{equation*}
    \Gamma^i_{jk}(v):=\gamma^i_{jk}(v) - \frac{1}{2} g^{il}(v) \left(\frac{\partial g_{lk}}{\partial v^m} N^m_j + \frac{\partial g_{jl}}{\partial v^m} N^m_k - \frac{\partial g_{jk}}{\partial v^m} N^m_l \right)(v).
\end{equation*}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rm:regular}
Suppose that $(M,L)$ is a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime.
Invoking Euler's homogeneous function theorem, we see that the spray coefficients $G^{i}$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{def:SprayCoeffImproved}
    G^i(v)= g^{il}(v) \left( \frac{\partial^2 L}{\partial x^j \partial v^l}(v)v^j- \frac{\partial L}{\partial x^l}(v)\right).
\end{equation}
This allows us to define the nonlinear connection coefficients $N^{i}_j$ even in this regularity, as $\partial_x^{\beta} \partial_v^{\sigma} L$ is well-defined and continuous for all $|\beta| \leq 1, |\sigma| \leq 3-|\beta|$.
For the convenience of the reader, we summarize the regularities of the various objects associated with $L \in C^{(1,2)}$:
\begin{align*}
    g_{ij},\, g^{ij} &\in C^{(1,0)}(TM \setminus \mathbf{0}),\\
    G^{i} &\in C^{(0,1)}(TM \setminus \mathbf{0}),\\
    \gamma^i_{jk},\,N^i_j,\, \Gamma^i_{jk} &\in C^{(0,0)}(TM \setminus \mathbf{0}).
\end{align*}
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ Finsler spacetime with $k \geq 1$ and $l \geq 2$.
Let $Y = Y^i \partial_{x^i},V = V^i\partial_{x^i}$ and $W = W^i \partial_{x^i} \neq 0$ be vector fields on $M$ of regularity $C^{m_1,\alpha_1}, C^{m_2,\alpha_2}$ and $C^{m_3,\alpha_3}$, respectively, where $m_1 \geq 1$.
Then the \emph{covariant derivative} of $Y$ in the direction $V$ with respect to \emph{reference direction} $W$ is defined as
\begin{equation*}
    D^W_V Y := \left( V^j \frac{\partial Y^i}{\partial x^j} + \Gamma^i_{jk}(W) Y^j V^k\right) \frac{\partial}{\partial x^i}.
\end{equation*}
Evidently, it is a vector field of regularity $C^{\min(k-1,l-2, m_1-1, m_2,m_3),\min(\alpha,\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\alpha_3)}$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
A $C^2$-curve $\eta:I \to M$ with an interval $I \subseteq \mathbb R$ is called a \emph{pregeodesic} if
\begin{equation*}
\Ddot{\eta}^i + \gamma^i_{jk}(\dot{\eta})\dot \eta^j \dot \eta^k = \lambda \dot{\eta}^i
\end{equation*}
on $I$ for some continuous function $\lambda:I \to \mathbb R$. It is called a \emph{geodesic} if $\lambda \equiv 0$. 
We may equivalently characterize (pre)geodesics via the Chern connection, i.e., $D^{\dot{\eta}}_{\dot{\eta}}\dot{\eta} =\lambda \dot{\eta}$ or $D^{\dot{\eta}}_{\dot{\eta}}\dot{\eta} = 0$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
Geodesics in a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime have fixed causal character, i.e., they are either timelike, null, or spacelike, on the entirety of their domain.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{Prop: Existence time for Leps geodesics}
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime and $(L_\varepsilon)_{\varepsilon >0}$ be smooth Lorentz--Finsler structures converging in $C^{(1,2)}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ to $L$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$.
Let $\mathcal K \subseteq TM$ be compact and suppose that all $L$-geodesics with initial data in $\mathcal{K}$ are defined on $[0,\infty)$.
Then for any $T>0$ there exists $\varepsilon_0=\varepsilon_0(\mathcal{K},T)>0$ such that for any $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0)$, all $L_\varepsilon$-geodesics with initial data in $\mathcal{K}$ are defined at least on $[0,T]$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Suppose that the claim is false, so that there is some $T>0$ as well as a sequence $\varepsilon_i \to 0$ and $v_i \in \mathcal K$ such that there are $L_{\varepsilon_i}$-geodesics $\eta_i$ with $\dot{\eta}_i(0) = v_i$ that are defined at most on $[0,T)$.
    By compactness, we may assume $v_i \to v \in \mathcal K$.
    Since the coefficients $\gamma^j_{kl}[L_{\varepsilon_i}]$ corresponding to $L_{\varepsilon_i}$ converge locally uniformly to the coefficients $\gamma^j_{kl}[L]$ of $L$, $\eta_i$ converges in $C^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$ to a solution $\eta$ of the $L$-geodesic equation with $\dot{\eta}(0) = v$.
    Then, an estimate of Hartman (see \cite[Prop.\ 2.5]{KOSS}) asserts that $\eta$ is defined at most on $[0,T)$, contradicting our assumption.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cr:Fcpt}
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime and $(L_\varepsilon)_{\varepsilon>0}$ be smooth Lorentz--Finsler structures converging in $C^{(1,2)}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ to $L$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$.
Let $\mathcal K \subseteq TM$ be compact and suppose that all $L$-geodesics with initial data in $\mathcal{K}$ are defined on $[0,T)$, with $T \leq +\infty$.
Then for any $\widetilde{T} \in [0,T)$ there exists $\varepsilon_0=\varepsilon_0(\mathcal{K},\widetilde{T}) > 0$ such that the set
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{K}, \widetilde{T}, \varepsilon_0}
    := \bigl\{ \dot{\eta}(t) : \eta \text{ is an } L_{\varepsilon}\text{-geodesic},\, \dot{\eta}(0) \in \mathcal K,\, \varepsilon \in [0,\varepsilon_0],\, t \in [0,\widetilde{T}] \bigr\}
    \subseteq TM
\end{equation*}
is compact, where we set $L_0 := L$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{Definition: distributional strong energy}
We say that a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime $(M,L)$ satisfies the \emph{distributional strong energy condition} if $\mathrm{Ric} \geq 0$ on $\Omega^T$ in the distributional sense, i.e., $\langle \mathrm{Ric}, \mu\rangle \geq 0$ for any nonnegative volume density $\mu$ on $TM$ compactly supported in $\Omega^T$.
Similarly, we say that $\mathrm{Ric} \geq K$ \emph{timelike distributionally} for $K \in \mathbb R$ if the distribution $\mathrm{Ric} + 2 K L$ is nonnegative.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
It is easily seen that this condition coincides with the usual strong energy condition in the case where $\mathrm{Ric} : TM \setminus \mathbf{0} \rightarrow \mathbb R$ is continuous.
    Indeed, if $\mathrm{Ric}(v) \geq 0$ for all $v \in \Omega^T$, then clearly $\langle \mathrm{Ric}, \mu \rangle \geq 0$ for any $\mu$ compactly supported in $\Omega^T$.
    On the other hand, if $\mathrm{Ric}(v)<0$ for some $v \in \Omega^T$, by continuity, this inequality continues to hold in a neighborhood $\mathcal{U} \subseteq TM$ of $v$ and by the openness of $\Omega^T$, we can assume that $\mathcal{U} \subseteq \Omega^T$.
    Hence, $\langle \mathrm{Ric}, \mu \rangle <0$ for any nonnegative, nontrivial $\mu$ compactly supported in $\mathcal{U}$. 
    The same argument shows that the general bound $\mathrm{Ric} \geq K$ agrees with its continuous counterpart if $\mathrm{Ric}$ is continuous.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{Definition: null energy condition}
    We say that a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime $(M,L)$ satisfies the \textit{distributional null energy condition} if for any compact set $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \liminf_{\delta \to 0} \inf_{\mu \in M_\delta(\mathcal{K})} \langle \mathrm{Ric}, \mu\rangle \geq 0,
    \end{equation*}
    where $M_\delta(\mathcal{K})$ is the set of densities $\mu$ on $TM$ such that $\mu \geq 0$, $\operatorname{supp} \mu \subseteq \{|L| \leq \delta \} \cap \Omega^C \cap \mathcal{K}$, and $\int_{TM} \mu = 1$. 
    When $M_\delta(\mathcal{K}) = \varnothing$, the infimum is set to $+ \infty$.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
Again, this is consistent with the usual definition in the smooth case. 
    Suppose that the Ricci curvature $\mathrm{Ric} : TM \setminus \mathbf{0} \rightarrow \mathbb R$ is continuous and satisfies the pointwise null energy condition $\mathrm{Ric}(v) \geq 0$ for all future null vectors $v \in TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$. 
    By continuity, for any $\varepsilon >0$, there exists an open neighborhood $\mathcal{U} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$ containing $\{ L = 0\} \cap \Omega^C$ such that $\mathrm{Ric}(v) > - \varepsilon$ for all $v \in \mathcal{U}$. 
    Now, for any compact set $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$, there exists $\delta_0 > 0$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \{|L| \leq \delta\} \cap \Omega^C \cap \mathcal{K} \subseteq \mathcal{U} \quad \textnormal{for all } \delta \in (0,\delta_0].
    \end{equation*}
    In fact, otherwise, the family $\mathcal{F}_\delta:=(\{|L| \leq \delta\} \cap \Omega^C \cap \mathcal{K}) \setminus \mathcal{U}$ constitutes a family of closed, nested, and nonempty sets, and hence, by the compactness of $\mathcal{K}$, we would have $\bigcap_{\delta >0} \mathcal{F}_\delta \neq \varnothing$. 
    This, however, contradicts $\bigcap_{\delta > 0} \{|L| \leq \delta\} \cap \Omega^C = \{L=0\} \cap \Omega^C \subseteq \mathcal{U}$.
    Therefore $\mathrm{Ric} > - \varepsilon$ on the support of any $\mu \in M_\delta(\mathcal{K})$, thereby
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle \mathrm{Ric}, \mu \rangle > - \varepsilon \int_{\{|L| \leq \delta\} \cap \Omega^C \cap \mathcal{K}} \mu = -\varepsilon \quad \textnormal{for any } \mu \in M_\delta(\mathcal{K}),\, \delta \in (0,\delta_0].
    \end{equation*}
    Next, suppose that there is some future null vector $v_0 \in TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$ such that $\mathrm{Ric}(v_0) < 0$. 
    Then there exists some $\varepsilon >0$ and a compact neighborhood $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$ of $v_0$ such that $\mathrm{Ric}(v) \leq - \varepsilon$ for all $v \in \mathcal{K}$. 
    Hence, $\langle \mathrm{Ric}, \mu \rangle \leq -\varepsilon$ for any $\delta >0$ and any $\mu \in M_\delta(\mathcal{K})$.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
If $L \in C^{(2,2)}$ and $\mathfrak m$ is a $C^2$-measure, then the above definition of weighted Ricci curvature agrees with the following usual one (see \cite{LuMinguzziOhtaSingularity,Ohta2021}).
Fix $v \in \Omega_x^T$, let $\eta:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon) \to M$ be the geodesic with $\dot{\eta}(0)=v$, and put $\psi_\eta(t):=\Psi(\eta(t),\dot{\eta}(t))$.
Then, since $\Psi \in C^{(2,0)}$ and $\eta \in C^3$, we can define
\[
\mathrm{Ric}^N_{\mathfrak m}(v) :=\mathrm{Ric}(v) +\psi''_\eta(0) -\frac{\psi'_\eta(0)^2}{N-n}.
\]
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
\label{Definition: Weighted energy conditions}
    Let $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime $(M,L)$ equipped with a $C^1$ measure $\mathfrak m$. 
    Recall $n=\dim M$.
\begin{enumerate}
\refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}[(1)] 
    Let $N \in (-\infty,1] \cup [n,\infty]$. We say that $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ satisfies $\mathrm{Ric}^N_{\mathfrak{m}} \geq K$ \emph{timelike distributionally} for $K \in \mathbb R$ (or the \emph{distributional strong $N$-energy condition} when $K=0$) if $\langle \mathrm{Ric}^N_{\mathfrak m} + 2 KL, \mu \rangle \geq 0$ for any nonnegative volume density $\mu$ compactly supported in $\Omega^T$. 
\refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}[(2)]
    Let $N \in (-\infty,2] \cup [n,\infty]$ with $n \ge 3$. 
    We say that $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ satisfies the \emph{distributional null $N$-energy condition} if for any compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf 0$ we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \liminf_{\delta \to 0} \inf_{\mu \in M_\delta(\mathcal K)} \langle \mathrm{Ric}^N_{\mathfrak m},\mu \rangle \geq 0,
    \end{equation*}
    where $M_\delta(\mathcal{K})$ is as in Definition \ref{Definition: null energy condition}.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime. If $L$ satisfies $\mathrm{Ric} \geq K$ timelike distributionally in the sense of Definition \ref{Definition: distributional strong energy} for some $K \in \mathbb R$, then it satisfies the distributional null energy condition. 
Similarly, in the weighted case, if $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ is a $C^1$ weighted $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime, $N \in (-\infty,1] \cup [n,\infty]$, $K \in \mathbb R$ and $\mathrm{Ric}^N_\mathfrak m \geq K$ timelike distributionally, then $(M,L, \mathfrak m)$ satisfies the distributional $N$-null energy condition. 
This was unclear even in the $C^1$ Lorentzian case \cite{Graf-SingularityC1}, however, we can establish it here due to our definition of (weighted) null energy condition, which is implied by \cite[Def.\ 5.1]{Graf-SingularityC1} for $C^1$ Lorentzian metrics and is sufficient for the purposes of establishing the Penrose singularity theorems (see Theorems \ref{theorem: unweighted penrose} and \ref{theorem: weighted penrose}).

Let us sketch the argument in the unweighted case with $K = 0$, the (un)weighted case with general $K$ is analogous. 
Suppose that $(M,L)$ is $C^{(1,2)}$ and satisfies the distributional strong energy condition. 
Fix a compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf 0$ and let $\mu \in M_\delta(\mathcal K)$, i.e., $\mu$ is supported in $\{|L| \leq \delta\} \cap \Omega^C \cap \mathcal K$. 
Let $X$ be the smooth time orientation vector field of $(M,L)$ and $s > 0$. Define the map $F_s:TM \to TM$ via $F_s(v):= v + sX(\pi(v))$.
Then the push-forward $\mu_s :=(F_s)_* \mu$ is a nonnegative volume density with compact support such that $\mathrm{supp}(\mu_s) = F_s(\mathrm{supp}(\mu)) \subseteq \Omega^T$. 
Since $\mu_s \to \mu$ in the sense of compactly supported volume densities, we conclude that
\begin{equation*}
    \langle \mathrm{Ric},\mu\rangle = \lim_{s \to 0} \langle \mathrm{Ric},\mu_s\rangle \geq 0.
\end{equation*}
Taking the infimum over $\mu \in M_\delta(\mathcal K)$ and then sending $\delta \downarrow 0$, we conclude that the distributional null energy condition is satisfied.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rmk:WeightedRicciSimplified}
    For the convergence results of the next section, it will be beneficial to rewrite the expression for $\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N$; cf.\ \cite[Sec.\ 7]{ShenSCurvature}, \cite[Lem.\ 10.6]{Ohta2021}.
    To do so, decompose $\Psi$ into
    \begin{equation*}
        \tau(x,v) := \frac{1}{2} \log \bigl|\det \bigl(g_{ij}(x,v)\bigr)\bigr|, \quad
        u(x):= \log \sigma(x),
    \end{equation*}
    and recall from \eqref{def:SprayCoeffImproved} that
    \begin{equation*}
        G^i = g^{il} A_l, \quad \textnormal{where} \, A_l := v^j \frac{\partial^2 L}{\partial x^j \partial v^l} - \frac{\partial L}{\partial x^l}.
    \end{equation*}
    Using the symmetry of $g^{il}$ and Jacobi's formula $2 \frac{\partial \tau}{\partial x^j} = g^{il}\frac{\partial g_{il}}{\partial x^j}$, we get
    \begin{align*}
        g^{il}\frac{\partial A_l}{\partial v^i} 
        = g^{il}\left(\frac{\partial^2L}{\partial x^i\partial v^l} +v^j\frac{\partial g_{il}}{\partial x^j} -\frac{\partial^2L}{\partial v^i \partial x^l} \right)
        = g^{il}v^j\frac{\partial g_{il}}{\partial x^j} = 2v^j\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial x^j}.
    \end{align*}
    Moreover, differentiating the inverse
    matrix $\partial_{v^i} g^{il} = - g^{ij} (\partial_{v^i}g_{jk})g^{kl}$ and using the symmetry of the vertical derivatives of
    $L$, we obtain
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\partial g^{il}}{\partial v^i}A_l = -g^{ij}\frac{\partial g_{jk}}{\partial v^i}G^k = -g^{ij}\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial v^k}G^k = -2G^k\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial v^k}.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence,
    \begin{equation*}
        \nu :=N^i_i = \frac{1}{2} \frac{\partial G^i}{\partial v^i}= v^j\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial x^j}
        -G^j\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial v^j} = S\tau.
    \end{equation*}
    Using this, we can rewrite the expression for $\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N$ as follows:
    Since $\Psi = \tau - u$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        S\Psi = \nu - Su = \nu - v^i \frac{\partial u}{\partial x^i}, \qquad S^2\Psi = S\nu -  v^iv^j \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^i \partial x^j} + G^i \frac{\partial u}{\partial x^i}.
    \end{equation*}
    Taking the trace of $R^i_j$ in \eqref{eq:DistributionalCurvature}, we find
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{Ric} = \frac{\partial G^i}{\partial x^i} - S \nu - N^i_k N^k_i.
    \end{equation*}
    Thus,
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:WeightedRicciSimplified}
        \mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N =\frac{\partial G^i}{\partial x^i} - N^i_j N^j_i- v^iv^j\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^i\partial x^j} +G^i\frac{\partial u}{\partial x^i} -\frac{1}{N-n}\left(N^i_i-v^i\frac{\partial u}{\partial x^i}\right)^2,
    \end{equation}
    where the last term is understood as $0$ when $N=\infty$.
    Notice that, in this expression, the term $S\nu$ in the definition of $\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N$ has canceled with the term $S^2\tau = S \nu$ coming from the distortion, and hence, no derivatives of $N^i_i$ appear in \eqref{eq:WeightedRicciSimplified}.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:PropertiesApproximation}
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ Finsler spacetime.
\begin{enumerate}
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}
    \label{PropApproximation1}
    We have $\mathcal{H}_\varepsilon L \to L$ in $C^{(k,l)}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ as $\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$, and when $\alpha >0$, in $C^{(k,l), \alpha'}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ \textnormal{(}in the sense of \eqref{eq:kla-conv}\textnormal{)} for every $\alpha' < \alpha$.
    Moreover, provided $\alpha>0$, for each compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf 0$, there exist $\varepsilon_\mathcal K, c_\mathcal K > 0$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \|\partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma \mathcal{H}_\varepsilon L - \partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma L\|_{\infty, \mathcal{K}} \leq c_\mathcal K \varepsilon^{\alpha} \quad \text{for all } \varepsilon \in (0, \varepsilon_\mathcal{K}]
    \end{equation*}
    and $|\beta| \leq k, \, |\sigma | \leq k+l-|\beta|$.
    In particular, if $L \in C^{(k,l)}$ with $k \geq 1$, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \|\partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma \mathcal{H}_\varepsilon L - \partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma L\|_{\infty, \mathcal{K}} \leq c_\mathcal K \varepsilon \quad \text{for all } \varepsilon \in (0, \varepsilon_\mathcal{K}]
    \end{equation*}
    and $|\beta| \leq k-1$, $|\sigma | \leq k+l-1-|\beta|$.
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} \label{PropApproximation2}
    There exists a smooth function $(\varepsilon,v) \mapsto L_{\varepsilon}(v)$, $(0,1) \times (TM \setminus \mathbf 0) \to \mathbb R$, such that
    \begin{enumerate}
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} each $v \mapsto L_\varepsilon(v)$ is a smooth Lorentz--Finsler structure, time-orientable by the same vector field as $L$;
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} for each compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf 0$, there exists $\varepsilon_{\mathcal K}>0$ such that $L_\varepsilon|_{\mathcal K} = \mathcal{H}_\varepsilon L|_{\mathcal K}$ for all $\varepsilon \in (0, \varepsilon_{\mathcal K}]$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
\ref{PropApproximation1}
For $\ell_a \star \rho_\varepsilon \to \ell_a$, these assertions follow from properties of the convolution, noting that $\| f - f\star\rho_\varepsilon\|_{\infty} \leq \| f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}} \varepsilon^\alpha$ provided that $f\colon \mathbb R^n \times \mathbb R^n \rightarrow \mathbb R$ is $\alpha$-H\"older continuous; see \cite[Proposition 3.5]{Graf-SingularityC1} for details. 
Now, to pass from $\ell_a \star \rho_\varepsilon$ to $\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon, a}L$, we make use of the homogeneity of $\ell_a$ to write
\begin{align*}
    &\partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma \mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon, a}L(x,v) - \partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma \ell_a(x,v) \\
    &= \partial_x^\beta \partial_v^\sigma \biggl[ r_a(x,v)^2 \biggl((\ell_a \star \rho_\varepsilon)\biggl(x,\frac{v}{r_a(x,v)}\biggr) -  \ell_a\biggl(x,\frac{v}{r_a(x,v)}\biggr)\biggr) \biggr],
\end{align*}
and note that $r_a(x,v)^2$ and $v/r_a(x,v)$ are smooth on $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$. 
Hence, by the product and chain rules, the above estimates pass to $\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}L$.

\ref{PropApproximation2}
Let $X$ be the timelike vector field determining the time orientation of $L$. 
For each compact set $ B \subseteq M$, the $\tilde g$-unit tangent bundle $\mathbb S_B$ over $B$ is compact. 
From the local uniform convergence of $\mathcal H_\varepsilon L$ and its second $v$-derivatives to those of $L$ established in \ref{PropApproximation1}, it follows that there exists $\varepsilon_B > 0$ such that, for every $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_B]$, the symmetric matrix $\partial_{v^i}\partial_{v^j}\mathcal H_\varepsilon L$ has Lorentzian signature on $\mathbb{S}_B$, as well as $\mathcal H_\varepsilon L(X(x)) < 0$ for every $x \in B$. 
Since $\mathcal H_\varepsilon L$ is positively $2$-homogeneous, its vertical Hessian is positively $0$-homogeneous. 
Hence, the signature assertion holds on all of $\pi^{-1}(B)\setminus \mathbf 0$.

Using the parameter construction in the proof of the \emph{globalization lemma} \cite[Lem.\ 4.3]{HoermannKunzingerSteinbauer-WaveEq}, with a compact exhaustion of $M$, we can therefore choose a smooth function $\mu : (0,1)\times M \rightarrow (0,1)$ such that, for every $(\varepsilon,x)$, the function $\mathcal{H}_{\mu(\varepsilon,x)}L$ has these properties on $T_xM\setminus\{0\}$, and such that $\mu(\varepsilon,x)=\varepsilon$ on a neighborhood of each fixed compact subset of $M$ for sufficiently small $\varepsilon >0$.
Set
\[
    L_\varepsilon(v)
    := \mathcal{H}_{\mu(\varepsilon,\pi(v))}L(v).
\]
This assignment is smooth away from the zero section and positively $2$-homogeneous. 
Moreover, since $\mu$ depends only on the base point,
\[
    \partial_{v^i}\partial_{v^j}L_\varepsilon(v)
    =            \partial_{v^i}\partial_{v^j}\mathcal{H}_{\mu(\varepsilon,x)}L(v),
    \quad x=\pi(v).
\]
Thus, $L_\varepsilon$ has Lorentzian signature and is time-oriented by $X$, proving (a). 
For a compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq TM\setminus \mathbf 0$, eventually $\mu(\varepsilon,\cdot)=\varepsilon$ on a neighborhood of $\pi(\mathcal K)$, thus $L_\varepsilon=\mathcal H_\varepsilon L$ on a neighborhood of $\mathcal K$, proving (b).
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{Remark: general semi Riemann}
A majority of the regularization results we have presented so far apply to any signature.
One may define a \emph{semi-Riemann--Finsler structure} of regularity $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ for $k + l \geq 2$ and $\alpha \in [0,1]$ in the same way as a Lorentz--Finsler structure in Definition \ref{Definition: Lorentz Finsler structure}, with the only difference being that the signature of $\partial^2 L/\partial v^i \partial v^j$ can be of general semi-Riemannian type $(-,\dots,-,+,\dots,+)$ with $s_-$ minuses and $s_+$ pluses, $s_- + s_+ = n$.
The case $s_- = 1$ corresponds to the Lorentz--Finsler setting, $s_- = 0$ to the Riemann--Finsler setting.

Given a $C^{(k,l),\alpha}$ semi-Riemann--Finsler structure $L$, one can construct the approximations $\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}L$ and $L_\varepsilon$, which have the properties described in Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation}, upon replacing all instances of ``Lorentz'' with ``semi-Riemann''.
Concerning the (weighted) Ricci curvature, the convergence is as good as in the Lorentzian case, namely
\begin{equation*}
    \mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon] -\mathcal{H}_\varepsilon \mathrm{Ric}[L] \to 0
\end{equation*}
locally uniformly on $TM\setminus \mathbf 0$ if $L \in C^{(1,3)}$, which is proven along the same lines used in the proof of Proposition \ref{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound} below.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- definition-12: body_md -->
\label{df:order}
We write $L_1 \prec L_2$, for two (not necessarily smooth) Lorentz--Finsler structures with time-orientation, if any future causal vector for $L_1$ is future timelike for $L_2$, i.e., if the future light cone of $L_2$ is ``wider" than that of $L_1$.
<!-- end definition-12 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:ChruscielGrantApprox}
Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime.
Then there exist smooth Lorentz--Finsler structures $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon$ and $\widehat{L}_\varepsilon$ on $M$, for $\varepsilon>0$, time-oriented by the same vector field as $L$, such that
\begin{enumerate}
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} \label{CG1}
    $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon \prec L \prec \widehat{L}_\varepsilon$;
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} \label{CG2}
    $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon - L_\varepsilon \to 0$ and $\widehat{L}_\varepsilon - L_\varepsilon \to 0$ in $C^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(TM \setminus \mathbf 0)$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$;
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} \label{CG3}
    for any compact set $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$, there exist $c_{\mathcal{K}} >0$ and $\varepsilon_0(\mathcal{K})>0$ such that, for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0(\mathcal{K}))$, the fundamental tensors of $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon$ and $L_\varepsilon$ satisfy
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:EstimatesInverseMetric}
        \| \check{g}_\varepsilon- g_\varepsilon \|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}} \leq c_{\mathcal{K}} \varepsilon, \qquad \| \check{g}_\varepsilon^{-1} - g_\varepsilon^{-1} \|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}} \leq c_{\mathcal{K}} \varepsilon,
    \end{equation}
    and we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:EstimatesCheckSpray}
        \| \widecheck{G}_{\varepsilon}^{i} - G_\varepsilon^{i} \|_{\infty, \mathcal{K}} \leq c_\mathcal{K} \varepsilon, \qquad \| (\widecheck{N}_{\varepsilon})^{i}_j - (N_\varepsilon)^{i}_j \|_{\infty, \mathcal{K}} \leq c_\mathcal{K} \varepsilon
    \end{equation}
    for the spray coefficients and the nonlinear connection coefficients.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
We will only construct $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon$; the construction of $\widehat{L}_\varepsilon$ is analogous.
Let $X$ be a smooth timelike vector field determining the time orientation of $(M,L)$. We cover $M$ by a locally finite family of charts $\{(U_a, \psi_a)\}_{a \in A}$ with relatively compact domains, and let $\{\xi_a\}_{a \in A}$ denote a respective partition of unity.

Now, fix a chart $(U_a,\psi_a)$ and assume that $X^n \neq 0$ in $U_a$. 
We set
\begin{equation*}
    \nu_a^{i} :=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} x^{i} - \frac{X^{i}}{X^n} \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} x^{n}
\end{equation*}
for $1 \leq i \leq n-1$.
Then, $\{\nu^1_a, \dots, \nu^{n-1}_a, \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} x^n\}$ is a smooth coframe on $U_a$ such that $\nu^i_{a}(X) = 0$ for all $i$, and $\nu_a(w):=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} \nu_a^i (w)^2$, $w \in TU_a$, is $2$-homogeneous with $\nu_{a}(w)=0$ precisely when $w$ is proportional to $X$.
By the relative compactness of $U_a$, the function $\nu_a$ attains the minimum value $\kappa_a$ on the compact set 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{V}_a:=\{w \in T\overline{U}\!_a  \cap (\Omega^C \setminus \Omega^T): |w|_{\tilde g} = 1\},
\end{equation*}
where $\tilde g$ is again an arbitrary Riemannian background metric.
This constant $\kappa_a$ is positive, as $\nu_a(w) = 0$ for some $w \in \Omega_x^C \setminus \Omega_x^T$ implies that $w = c X(x)$ for some $c >0$ (both are future-directed), but then $L(w) = c^2 L(X(x)) < 0$, a contradiction.

Moreover, by Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation}, there exist constants $c_a,\varepsilon_a>0$ such that $|L_\varepsilon(w) - L(w)| \leq c_a \varepsilon$ for any $w \in \mathcal{V}_a$ and $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_a]$.
Choosing a constant $C_a > c_a/\kappa_a$, we now set
\begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{L}_\varepsilon := L_\varepsilon + \varepsilon \sum_{a \in A} C_a\xi_a(\pi) \nu_{a}.
\end{equation*}
We claim that $\widetilde{L}_\varepsilon \prec L$ on $T \overline{U}\!_a$ for $\varepsilon \in (0, \varepsilon_a]$. 
To this end, let $w \in T \overline{U}\!_a \cap (\Omega^C \setminus \Omega ^T)$ and suppose, without loss of generality, that $|w|_{\tilde g} = 1$ (i.e., $w \in \mathcal{V}_a$).
Since
\begin{equation*}
    L_\varepsilon(w) = \sum_{a \in A} \xi_a \bigl( \pi(w) \bigr) \bigl( L_\varepsilon(w) - L(w)\bigr) \\
    \geq - \varepsilon \sum_{a \in A} \xi_a \bigl( \pi(w) \bigr)c_a
\end{equation*}
for $\varepsilon \in (0, \varepsilon_a]$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{L}_\varepsilon(w) = L_\varepsilon(w) + \varepsilon \sum_{a \in A} C_a\xi_a \bigl(\pi(w) \bigr) \nu_a(w) \geq \varepsilon \sum_{a \in A} \xi_a \bigl(\pi(w)\bigr) \bigl(C_a\nu_a(w) - c_a \bigr) > 0,
\end{equation*}
implying that, indeed, $\widetilde{L}_\varepsilon \prec L$ on $T \overline{U}\!_a$ for $\varepsilon \in (0, \varepsilon_a]$.

Furthermore, setting $Q:=\sum_{a \in A} C_a\xi_a(\pi) \nu_{a}$, for any compact set $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$ and any $l \in \mathbb N$, there exist $c_{\mathcal{K},l},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{K},l}>0$ such that 
\begin{equation} \label{eq:CGProofLCheckLEps}
    \| \widetilde{L}_\varepsilon - L_\varepsilon\|_{C^l(\mathcal{K})} =  \varepsilon \| Q\|_{C^l(\mathcal{K})} \leq c_{\mathcal{K},l} \varepsilon
\end{equation}
for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_{\mathcal{K},l}]$.
Hence, $\widetilde{L}_\varepsilon - L_\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$ in $C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(TM \setminus \mathbf{0})$.
Then we also immediately see that $(\tilde g_\varepsilon)_{ij} = (g_\varepsilon)_{ij} + \varepsilon q_{ij}$, where $q_{ij}:=\partial_{v^i}\partial_{v^j} Q$, satisfies
\begin{equation} \label{eq:CGProofMetrics}
    \| \tilde g_\varepsilon - g_\varepsilon \|_{\infty, \mathcal{K}} \leq c_{\mathcal{K}} \varepsilon
\end{equation}
on any compact set $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$, for some $c_{\mathcal K},\varepsilon_{\mathcal K}>0$, and for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_{\mathcal K}]$.
This shows that $\tilde{g}_\varepsilon$ has the same signature as $g_\varepsilon$ on $\mathcal{K}$ for sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$.

We now use the same base-dependent globalization as in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation}\ref{PropApproximation2}.
For each compact $B \subseteq M$, apply the preceding estimates on the $\tilde g$-unit tangent bundle $\mathbb{S}_B$. 
By homogeneity, for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, the vertical Hessian of $\widetilde L_\varepsilon$ has Lorentzian signature on $\pi^{-1}(B)\setminus \mathbf 0$, and $\widetilde L_\varepsilon \prec L$ over $B$. 
Moreover, $X$ is $\widetilde{L}_\varepsilon$-timelike since $Q(X)=0$. The parameter construction in \cite[Lem.\ 4.3]{HoermannKunzingerSteinbauer-WaveEq}
therefore gives a smooth function $\mu:(0,1) \times M \to (0,1)$ such that
\[
    \widecheck L_\varepsilon(v)
    := \widetilde L_{\mu(\varepsilon,\pi(v))}(v)
\]
is a smooth Lorentz--Finsler structure time-oriented by $X$, with $\widecheck L_\varepsilon \prec L$ everywhere. 
As in Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation}\ref{PropApproximation2}, $\widecheck L_\varepsilon$ is positively $2$-homogeneous, and the vertical Hessian is obtained by evaluating that of $\widetilde L_{\mu(\varepsilon,x)}$ with $x=\pi(v)$.
Furthermore, for every compact $\mathcal K \subseteq TM\setminus \mathbf 0$, we have $\widecheck L_\varepsilon=\widetilde L_\varepsilon$ on a
neighborhood of $\mathcal K$ for sufficiently small $\varepsilon >0$.
This completes the construction in \ref{CG1}, and the convergence claim in \ref{CG2} now directly follows from \eqref{eq:CGProofLCheckLEps}.

For the estimates in \ref{CG3}, notice that \eqref{eq:CGProofMetrics} immediately gives the first estimate in \eqref{eq:EstimatesInverseMetric}.
Moreover, since $g_\varepsilon \rightarrow g$ locally uniformly and $g$ is non-degenerate, both $g_\varepsilon$ and $\check g_\varepsilon$ are uniformly non-degenerate on $\mathcal{K}$ for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_{\mathcal{K}}]$. 
In particular, their inverses are uniformly bounded on $\mathcal{K}$. 
Using
\begin{equation} \label{eq:matrixInversion}
    \check g_\varepsilon^{-1}-g_\varepsilon^{-1} = \check g_\varepsilon^{-1}(g_\varepsilon-\check g_\varepsilon)g_\varepsilon^{-1},
\end{equation}
we thus obtain
\begin{equation} \label{eq:CGProofInverse}
    \|\check g_\varepsilon^{-1}-g_\varepsilon^{-1}\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}} \leq c_{\mathcal{K}}\varepsilon.
\end{equation}
For the estimate of the spray coefficients, by \eqref{def:SprayCoeffImproved}, this amounts to analyzing the difference
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:G_eps}
    \widecheck G_\varepsilon^i -G_\varepsilon^i =( \check g_\varepsilon^{il}-g_\varepsilon^{il})
    (\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon)_l + g_\varepsilon^{il}\bigl( (\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon)_l - (A_\varepsilon)_l \bigr),
\end{equation} 
where $(A_\varepsilon)_l := v^j \partial_{x^j} \partial_{v^l} L_\varepsilon - \partial_{x^l} L_\varepsilon$ and correspondingly for $(\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l$.
The first term is $O(\varepsilon)$ thanks to \eqref{eq:CGProofInverse} and $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon \rightarrow L$ in $C^{(1,2)}_\mathrm{loc}(TM \setminus \mathbf{0})$, while the second term is $O(\varepsilon)$ by \eqref{eq:CGProofLCheckLEps} and the local uniform boundedness of $(g_\varepsilon)^{-1}$. 

Finally, to prove the same estimate for the nonlinear connection coefficients, we first claim that
\begin{equation*}
    \| \partial_v (\check g_\varepsilon^{-1}-g_\varepsilon^{-1}) \|_{\infty,\mathcal K}\leq c_{\mathcal K}\varepsilon.
\end{equation*}
For this, we use the identity
\begin{equation*}
    \check g_\varepsilon^{-1}-g_\varepsilon^{-1}
    = -\varepsilon \check g_\varepsilon^{-1} q g_\varepsilon^{-1}, \qquad q=(q_{ij}), 
\end{equation*}
following from $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon -L_\varepsilon = \varepsilon Q$, to get
\begin{equation*}
    \partial_{v}
    (\check g_\varepsilon^{-1}-g_\varepsilon^{-1}) 
    = -\varepsilon (\partial_{v}\check g_\varepsilon^{-1}) qg_\varepsilon^{-1} 
    -\varepsilon \check g_\varepsilon^{-1}(\partial_{v}q)g_\varepsilon^{-1}
    -\varepsilon \check g_\varepsilon^{-1}q(\partial_{v}g_\varepsilon^{-1}).
\end{equation*}
Now, all the terms appearing in this expression are uniformly bounded on $\mathcal K$; the inverse metrics are uniformly bounded by the argument above, $q$ and $\partial_v q$ are smooth, and $\partial_v g_\varepsilon^{-1}$, $\partial_v \check g_\varepsilon^{-1}$ are uniformly bounded by the $C^{(1,2)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(TM \setminus \mathbf{0})$-convergence of $L_\varepsilon$ and $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon$ to $L$.

Moreover, from \eqref{eq:CGProofLCheckLEps}, we get that $(\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon - A_\varepsilon)$ and $\partial_v (\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon - A_\varepsilon)$ are $O(\varepsilon)$ on $\mathcal{K}$, while both $\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon$ and $\partial_v \widecheck{A}_\varepsilon$ are uniformly bounded on $\mathcal{K}$ as $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon \rightarrow L$ in $C^{(1,2)}_\mathrm{loc}(TM \setminus \mathbf{0})$. 
Differentiating the expression \eqref{eq:G_eps} for
$\widecheck G_\varepsilon-G_\varepsilon$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ProofCGN}
\begin{split}
    \partial_{v^j} \bigl(\widecheck G_\varepsilon^i -G_\varepsilon^i \bigr)
    &= \partial_{v^j} (\check g_\varepsilon^{il}-g_\varepsilon^{il})
    (\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon)_l + (\check g_\varepsilon^{il}-g_\varepsilon^{il})
    \partial_{v^j} (\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon)_l \\
    &\quad + \partial_{v^j} g_\varepsilon^{il} \bigl( (\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon)_l - (A_\varepsilon)_l \bigr) + g_\varepsilon^{il} \partial_{v^j} \bigl((\widecheck{A}_\varepsilon)_l - (A_\varepsilon)_l\bigr).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The above arguments imply that each of these terms is $O(\varepsilon)$ on $\mathcal{K}$, and hence, as desired,
\begin{equation*}
    \|(\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_j-(N_\varepsilon)^i_j\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}}
    \leq c_{\mathcal{K}}\varepsilon. \qedhere
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:RicciCheckConvergence}
    Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime. Then, on any compact set $\mathcal{K} \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf{0}$, we have $\mathrm{Ric} [\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon] - \mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon] \rightarrow 0$ uniformly as $\varepsilon \to 0$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
In view of \eqref{eq:DistributionalCurvature}, we shall estimate the difference
\begin{align*}
    \mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] - \mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon]
    &= \partial_{x^i}(\widecheck G_\varepsilon^i-G_\varepsilon^i)
    - \partial_{x^k}\bigl((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_{i}-( N_\varepsilon)^i_{i}\bigr)v^k \\
    &\quad + \partial_{v^k}\bigl((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_{i}-(N_\varepsilon)^i_{i}\bigr)\widecheck G_\varepsilon^k 
    + \partial_{v^k}(N_\varepsilon)^i_{i}\bigl(\widecheck G_\varepsilon^k-G_\varepsilon^k\bigr)\\
    &\quad - \bigl((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_{k}(\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^k_{i}
    - (N_\varepsilon)^i_{k}(N_\varepsilon)^k_{i}\bigr).
\end{align*}
Our assumptions on the regularity of $L$ imply that $G^{i} \in C^{(0,1)}$ and $N^{i}_k \in C^{(0,0)}$; recall Remark \ref{rm:regular}. 
We first note that the last term 
\begin{equation*}
    (\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_{k}(\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^k_{i} 
    - (N_\varepsilon)^i_{k}(N_\varepsilon)^k_{i}
    = \bigl((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_{k}-(N_\varepsilon)^i_{k}\bigr)(\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^k_{i}
    + (N_\varepsilon)^i_{k}\bigl((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^k_{i}-(N_\varepsilon)^k_{i}\bigr)
\end{equation*}
tends to $0$ uniformly. 
This is because $N_\varepsilon, \widecheck N_\varepsilon$ are locally uniformly bounded and 
\begin{equation*}
    \|(\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_k -(N_\varepsilon)^i_k\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}} \leq c_{\mathcal{K}}\varepsilon
\end{equation*}
by the estimate \eqref{eq:EstimatesCheckSpray}. 

Next, we establish the convergence of the first three terms by showing
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:GNN}
    \|\partial_x(\widecheck G_\varepsilon-G_\varepsilon)\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}}
    +
    \|\partial_x(\widecheck N_\varepsilon-N_\varepsilon)\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}}
    +
    \|\partial_v(\widecheck N_\varepsilon-N_\varepsilon)\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}}
    \rightarrow 0.
\end{equation}
For the first term $\partial_{x^i} (\widecheck{G}^{i}_\varepsilon - G^{i}_\varepsilon)$, we can essentially follow the same argument we gave for the proof of the estimate on the nonlinear connection coefficients in \eqref{eq:ProofCGN} with the important exception that now $\partial_{x^i} (\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l$, where 
\begin{equation*}
    (\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l= v^j \partial_{x^j} \partial_{v^l} \widecheck L_\varepsilon - \partial_{x^l} \widecheck L_\varepsilon
\end{equation*}
as in the above proof, is \emph{not} locally bounded since $\partial_{x^j} \partial_{v^l} L \in C^{(0,1)}$. 
However, we will show that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eAto0}
    \varepsilon \|\partial_{x^i} (\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l\|_{\infty, \mathcal{K}} \rightarrow 0 \quad \textnormal{as} \quad \varepsilon \rightarrow 0,
\end{equation}
which is sufficient to conclude the convergence $\|\partial_{x^i}(\widecheck{G}_\varepsilon^{i} - G_\varepsilon^{i})\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}} \to 0$ by virtue of \eqref{eq:EstimatesInverseMetric}. 
We reduce the situation to a standard mollifier estimate on $\mathbb R^{2n}$ as follows. 
First, as $\widecheck L_\varepsilon = L_\varepsilon + \varepsilon Q$ on $\mathcal{K}$ (for small $\varepsilon$), with smooth $Q$ as in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:ChruscielGrantApprox}, it is sufficient to prove the claim for $L_\varepsilon$.
Furthermore, in local coordinates, we can disregard cut-off and transition functions appearing in the convolution formula \eqref{eq:HeaL}, as they do not spoil the convergence (the terms arising from differentiating the smooth cut-off and transition functions contain at most first derivatives of the regularized coefficients and are therefore locally uniformly bounded). 
Hence, it suffices to study
\begin{equation}\label{eq: ProofRicReduction}
    \partial_{x^i}(f \star \rho_\varepsilon),
\end{equation}
where $f$ is a continuous function on $\mathbb R^{2n}$ (representing either $\partial_{x^j} \partial_{v^l} L$ or $\partial_{x^l} L$). 
To prove this, using the integration by parts and $c \star \partial_{x^i}\rho_\varepsilon=0$ for any constant $c$, we write
\begin{align*}
    \partial_{x^i}(f \star \rho_\varepsilon)(z) 
    &=\int_{\mathbb R^{2n}} \partial_{x^i}f(z-y)\rho_\varepsilon(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y
    =\int_{\mathbb R^{2n}} f(z-y)\partial_{x^i}\rho_\varepsilon(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y \\ 
    &=\int_{\mathbb R^{2n}} \bigl(f(z-y)-f(z)\bigr)\partial_{x^i}\rho_\varepsilon(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y.
\end{align*}
Letting $\omega_K$ denote the modulus of continuity of $f$ on a compact set $K \subseteq \mathbb R^{2n}$ and recalling $\rho_\varepsilon(y)=\varepsilon^{-2n}\rho(y/\varepsilon)$, we have
\begin{alignat*}{2}
    |\varepsilon\partial_{x^i} (f \star \rho_\varepsilon)(z)|
    &\leq \varepsilon \int_{\mathbb R^{2n}}\omega_K(|y|)|\partial_{x^i}\rho_\varepsilon(y)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y 
    =\varepsilon^{-2n} \int_{\mathbb R^{2n}} \omega_K(|y|) |\partial_{x^i}\rho(y/\varepsilon)| \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y \\
    &= \int_{\mathbb R^{2n}}\omega_K(\varepsilon|y|)|\partial_{x^i}\rho(y)| \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y.
\end{alignat*}
Now, the right-hand side of this expression tends to $0$ by the dominated convergence theorem. 
Hence,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ConvergenceC0derivative}
    \varepsilon\|\partial_{x^i}(f \star \rho_\varepsilon)\|_{\infty,K}\rightarrow 0,
\end{equation}
giving the claim \eqref{eq:eAto0}. 

For the latter two terms in \eqref{eq:GNN}, we can apply a similar argument. 
Indeed, to control $\partial_{x^k} ((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_i - (N_\varepsilon)^i_i)$, the potentially problematic terms are (recall \eqref{eq:ProofCGN})
\begin{equation*}
    \partial_{v^i}(\check g_\varepsilon^{il} 
    - g_\varepsilon^{il}) \partial_{x^k} (\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l, \qquad 
    (\check g_\varepsilon^{il} - g_\varepsilon^{il})\partial_{x^k}\partial_{v^i} (\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l, \qquad 
    \partial_{x^k}\partial_{v^i} \check g_\varepsilon^{il}\bigl((\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l - (A_\varepsilon)_l\bigr). 
\end{equation*}
All of these are controlled exactly as in the estimate for $\partial_{x^i}(\widecheck G^i_\varepsilon-G^i_\varepsilon)$.
Indeed, we have already seen that the respective factors involving the differences of $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon$- and $L_\varepsilon$-quantities are $O(\varepsilon)$, while the other factors can be reduced to a problem of the form \eqref{eq: ProofRicReduction} again. 
For $\partial_{v^k} ((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_i - (N_\varepsilon)^i_i)$, the only two potentially problematic terms are
\begin{equation*}
    (\check g_\varepsilon^{il}
    -g_\varepsilon^{il})\partial_{v^k}\partial_{v^i} (\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l, \qquad \partial_{v^k} \partial_{v^i} g_\varepsilon^{il}\bigl((\widecheck A_\varepsilon)_l - (A_\varepsilon)_l\bigr)
\end{equation*}
which can again be handled as above. 

Repeating the same argument one last time to get $\varepsilon\|\partial_{v^k} (N_\varepsilon)^i_i\|_{\infty,\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow 0$, we have thus proven that
$\mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] - \mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon] \rightarrow 0$
uniformly on $\mathcal{K}$, as claimed.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound}
    Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,3)}$ Finsler spacetime satisfying the distributional strong energy condition, and let $\mathcal{K} \subseteq \Omega^T$ be compact.
    Then for any $\delta >0$, there exists $\varepsilon_0 =\varepsilon_0(\mathcal{K},\delta) >0$ such that for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0)$ and $v \in \mathcal{K}$ with $2 \widecheck{L}_\varepsilon(v)=-1$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon](v) \ge - \delta.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We show that $\mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] - \mathcal{H}_\varepsilon \mathrm{Ric}[L]$ tends to $0$ locally uniformly.
Then, as $\mathrm{Ric}[L] \geq 0$ on $\Omega^T$ in the distributional sense, we have $\mathcal{H}_\varepsilon \mathrm{Ric}[L]\geq 0$ on $\mathcal{K} \subseteq \Omega^T$ for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon >0$, giving the claim. 
To prove this convergence, we split the difference into two pieces:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] - \mathcal{H}_\varepsilon \mathrm{Ric}[L]= (\mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] - \mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon]) + (\mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon]-\mathcal{H}_\varepsilon \mathrm{Ric}[L]).
\end{equation*}
By Lemma \ref{lem:RicciCheckConvergence}, we know that $\mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon] - \mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon] \rightarrow 0$ locally uniformly, and thus the first piece tends to zero.
We shall show that the second piece also converges to zero.

For this, by homogeneity, it suffices to establish convergence on $\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}:= \{v/\|v\|_{\tilde g} : v \in \mathcal{K}\}$.
On a neighborhood of this set, for sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, we have $L_\varepsilon = \mathcal{H}_\varepsilon L$.
By compactness, we can restrict to a fixed chart $(U_a, \psi_a)$.
We now estimate the terms arising from homogenization.
Recalling \eqref{eq:HeaL}, for $(x,v) \in \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\psi_a(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})$ we have
\begin{align*}
&\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon,a} L(x,v) - (\ell_a \star \rho_\varepsilon)(x,v) \\
&= \int_{\mathbb R^n \times \mathbb R^n} \bigl\{ \ell_a\bigl( x-\varepsilon y,v-\varepsilon r_a(x,v)w \bigr) -\ell_a(x-\varepsilon y,v-\varepsilon w)\bigr\} \rho(y,w) \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} w.
\end{align*}
Hence, since $r_a(x,v)=1$,
\begin{align*}
&\partial_{x^i} \bigl( \mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon,a} L(x,v) - (\ell_a \star \rho_\varepsilon)(x,v) \bigr) \\
&= -\varepsilon \partial_{x^i}r_a(x,v) \int_{\mathbb R^n \times \mathbb R^n} w^j \partial_{v^j} \ell_a(x-\varepsilon y, v - \varepsilon w) \rho(y,w) \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} y \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} w.
\end{align*}
As $\ell_a \in C^{(1,3)}$, it follows that $\partial_{x^i} \mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon,a} L - \partial_{x^i}(\ell_a \star \rho_\varepsilon)$ is $O(\varepsilon)$. 
We can argue analogously for mixed derivatives $\partial^{\beta}_x \partial^{\sigma}_v$, provided $|\beta| \leq 1$ and $|\beta| + |\sigma| \leq 4$.
The case of two $x$-derivatives ($|\beta| = 2$, $|\sigma| \leq 2$) is handled as in the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:RicciCheckConvergence} using \eqref{eq:ConvergenceC0derivative}.
Hence, we may replace $\mathcal H_{\varepsilon,a}L$ by the pure convolution term $\ell_a\star\rho_\varepsilon$, up to an error converging to zero locally uniformly.

Now, recall that the coordinate expression for $\mathrm{Ric}[L]$ is given by
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{\partial G^i}{\partial x^i} - \frac{\partial N^i_i}{\partial x^k}v^k + \frac{\partial N^i_i}{\partial v^k}G^k - N^i_kN^k_i.
\end{equation*}
After expanding this, we see that the only terms containing second-order horizontal derivatives of $L$ are
\begin{equation*}
    g^{il} \partial_{x^i} A_l - \frac{1}{2}v^k \bigl( (\partial_{v^i}g^{il})\partial_{x^k}A_l + g^{il} \partial_{x^k} \partial_{v^i}A_l \bigr),
\end{equation*}
where we have used that $G^{i} = g^{il} A_l$ with $g^{il} \in C^{(1,1)}$ and $A_l := v^j \partial_{x^j} \partial_{v^l} L - \partial_{x^l} L \in C^{(0,2)}$, as well as $N_i^i = \frac{1}{2}((\partial_{v^i}g^{il})A_l + g^{il} \partial_{v^i}A_l) \in C^{(0,1)}$.
All the other terms contain at most first-order $x$-derivatives with total order at most $4$, hence the difference of these terms computed from $L_\varepsilon$ and $L$ converges locally uniformly to zero by Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation}\ref{PropApproximation1}.

For the terms containing second order $x$-derivatives, the key ingredient is the following \emph{Friedrichs-type commutator estimate}; cf.\ \cite[Lemma 4.9]{Graf-SingularityC1}:

Let $a_\varepsilon \in C^1(\mathbb R^{2n})$, for $\varepsilon \geq 0$, and suppose that $a_\varepsilon \rightarrow a_0$ in $C^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ as $\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$   with $\| a_\varepsilon - a_0\|_{\infty,K} \leq c_K \varepsilon $ on any compact set $K \subseteq \mathbb R^{2n}$ with some $c_K>0$.
Then, for any $f \in C(\mathbb R^{2n})$, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Friedrichs}
    \partial_{z}^\sigma\bigl(a_\varepsilon (f \star \rho_\varepsilon)\bigr) - \partial_{z}^\sigma\bigl((a_0f) \star \rho_\varepsilon\bigr) \rightarrow 0
\end{equation}
locally uniformly, where $z=(x,v)$, $|\sigma| \le 1$.
By the product rule and using $\partial_z^{\sigma} a_\varepsilon \rightarrow \partial_z^{\sigma} a_0$, this takes the more suggestive form
\begin{equation*}
    a_\varepsilon \partial_z^{\sigma} (f\star \rho_\varepsilon) - (a_0 \partial_z^{\sigma} f)\star \rho_\varepsilon\rightarrow 0,
\end{equation*}
where $\partial_z^{\sigma} f$ is interpreted distributionally.

We can apply this to our setting by noticing that $g^{il}_\varepsilon \rightarrow g^{il}$ and $\partial_{v^i}g_\varepsilon^{il} \rightarrow \partial_{v^i}g^{il}$ in $C^1_\mathrm{loc}$ (for the latter we need $L \in C^{(1,3)}$) with the norm of their difference being $O(\varepsilon)$ on any compact set, again by Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation} (and the formula for matrix inversion; cf.\ \eqref{eq:matrixInversion}).

Hence,
\begin{equation*}
    \mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon] - \sum_{a \in A} (\chi_a \circ \pi) (\psi_a)^{*} \bigl( (\psi_a)_*\bigl( (\zeta_a \circ \pi) \mathrm{Ric}[L] \bigr) \star \rho_\varepsilon \bigr) \rightarrow 0
\end{equation*}
locally uniformly on $\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}$.
On this set, the latter term agrees with $\mathcal{H}_\varepsilon \mathrm{Ric}[L]$ and by homogeneity, the convergence holds on all of $\mathcal K$, completing the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{rm:12alpha}
\begin{enumerate}
\refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}[(a)]
One can alternatively prove Proposition \ref{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound} for $L \in C^{(1,2),\alpha}$ with $\alpha > 1/2$. 
In this case $g^{ij}\in C^{(1,0),\alpha}, A_l\in C^{(0,1),\alpha}$.
Hence, the first term in
\[
2N^i_i =(\partial_{v^i} g^{il}) A_l +g^{il} \partial_{v^i} A_l
\]
is merely the product of two locally $\alpha$-H\"older continuous functions.
Locally on $\mathbb R^{2n}$, the corresponding terms in the difference $\mathrm{Ric}[L_\varepsilon] - \mathrm{Ric}[L]\star\rho_\varepsilon$ hence reduce to a differentiated commutator of the form
\begin{equation*}
    \partial_z^{\sigma} \bigl(a_\varepsilon(f \star \rho_\varepsilon) - (a_0f) \star \rho_\varepsilon \bigr)
\end{equation*}
where $z=(x,v)$, $| \sigma| \leq 1$ and $a_\varepsilon,f\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^{2n})$.
Here, the commutator estimate gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigl\|\partial_z^{\sigma} \bigl(a_\varepsilon(f \star \rho_\varepsilon) - (a_0f) \star \rho_\varepsilon \bigr)\bigr\|_{\infty, K} \leq c_K \varepsilon^{2 \alpha - 1}
    \end{equation*}
for any compact set $K \subseteq \mathbb R^{2n}$.
In particular, this applies to the commutator
\begin{equation*}
        \partial_{x^k} \Bigl( \bigl( (\partial_{v^i} g^{il}) \star \rho_\varepsilon \bigr) (A_l \star \rho_\varepsilon) -\bigl( (\partial_{v^i} g^{il}) A_l \bigr)\star \rho_\varepsilon \Bigr)
    \end{equation*}
    in the second term of the Ricci curvature.
    The commutators arising from the third term are treated in the same way after rewriting
    \begin{equation*}
        (\partial_{v^k} N_i^{i}) G^k = \partial_{v^k}(N_i^{i}G^k) - N_i^{i} \partial_{v^k} G^k,
    \end{equation*}
    where $N^i_i$ is $\alpha$-H\"older continuous.
    As $2 \alpha -1 >0$, all of these terms converge to zero as $\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$.
    The remaining terms are handled exactly as in the proof of Proposition \ref{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound}.

    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}[(b)]
    The condition $L \in C^{(1,2),\alpha}$ implies $g_{ij} \in C^{(1,0),\alpha}=C^{1,\alpha}$.
    Hence, for purely Lorentzian metrics $g$, this condition is stronger than $g \in C^{1}$; even though, for general $L$, it reduces the demands on vertical regularity.
    In this sense, these two conditions are independent.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:GeneralRicciBound}
    Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,3)}$ Finsler spacetime such that $\mathrm{Ric} \geq K$ timelike distributionally, where $K \in \mathbb R$. 
    Then for any compact set $\mathcal{K} \subseteq \Omega^T$ and $\delta > 0$, there exists $\varepsilon_0 = \varepsilon_0(\mathcal{K},\delta) > 0$ such that for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0)$ and $v \in \mathcal{K}$ with $2 \widecheck L_\varepsilon(v) = -1$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon](v) \geq K - \delta.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
By assumption $\mathrm{Ric}[L]+2KL\geq0$ timelike distributionally, thus
\[
    \mathcal H_\varepsilon\mathrm{Ric}[L]
    +2K \mathcal H_\varepsilon L \geq 0
\]
pointwise on $\mathcal K$ for $\varepsilon >0$
small enough, since the kernels defining $\mathcal H_\varepsilon$ are
nonnegative and supported in $\Omega^T$.
In the proof of
Proposition \ref{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound}, we have already established that
\[
    \operatorname{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon]
    -\mathcal H_\varepsilon\mathrm{Ric}[L]\rightarrow0
\]
locally uniformly.
Since also
$\widecheck L_\varepsilon-\mathcal H_\varepsilon L\to0$ locally uniformly by Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation}, the claim follows.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{Prop: Null approximate Ricci}
    Let $(M,L)$ be a $C^{(1,3)}$ Finsler spacetime satisfying the distributional null energy condition. 
    Then for any compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf 0$ and $\delta > 0$, there exists $\varepsilon_0 = \varepsilon_0(\mathcal K,\delta)>0$ such that for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0)$ and $v \in \mathcal{K} \cap \Omega^C[\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon]$ with $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon(v) = 0$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon](v) \ge - \delta.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
By positive $2$-homogeneity, it suffices to consider the case where $\mathcal K$ is contained in the $\tilde g$-unit tangent bundle over one of the relatively compact base neighborhoods, denoted by $U$, used in the construction of the approximation. 
For each small $\varepsilon>0$ and $v\in \mathcal K$, denote by $\mu_{\varepsilon,v}$ the nonnegative smooth test density on $\Omega^C$ for which
\[
    \mathcal H_\varepsilon\mathrm{Ric}[L](v)
    =
    \langle\mathrm{Ric}[L],\mu_{\varepsilon,v}\rangle.
\]
Its total mass
\[
    a_{\varepsilon,v}:=\int_{TM}\mu_{\varepsilon,v}
\]
converges uniformly to $1$ on $\mathcal K$ due to the partition of unity construction.
Let $\mathcal K'\subseteq TM\setminus \mathbf 0$ be a compact set containing all these supports for sufficiently small $\varepsilon$.
We claim that there exists $c>0$ such that
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{\mu_{\varepsilon,v}}{a_{\varepsilon,v}}
    \in M_{c\varepsilon}(\mathcal K')
    \qquad
    \text{for all }v\in \mathcal{K} \cap\Omega^C[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] \cap \widecheck L_\varepsilon^{-1}(0).
\end{equation*}
Indeed, Proposition \ref{prop:PropertiesApproximation} and the local Lipschitz continuity of $L$ give constants $A,B>0$ such that
\[
    |L_\varepsilon(v)-L(v)|\leq A\varepsilon,
    \qquad
    |L(w)-L(v)|\leq B\varepsilon
    \quad\text{for }w\in\operatorname{supp}\mu_{\varepsilon,v}.
\]
The functions $\nu_a$ used in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:ChruscielGrantApprox} are strictly positive on $\tilde g$-unit future $L$-null vectors in their respective charts.
Possibly choosing larger constants $C_a$ in the construction of $Q$ (cf.\ again the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:ChruscielGrantApprox}), we can ensure that
\[
    Q(v)\geq A+B+1
\]
on a neighborhood of future $\tilde g$-unit null vectors with basepoints in $U$.
Since $\widecheck L_\varepsilon\to L$, the $\tilde g$-unit future $\widecheck L_\varepsilon$-null vectors are contained in that neighborhood for sufficiently small $\varepsilon > 0$. 
For such a vector $v$, using $\widecheck L_\varepsilon=L_\varepsilon+\varepsilon Q$, we obtain
\[
    L(v)
    \leq L_\varepsilon(v)+A\varepsilon
    =-\varepsilon\bigl(Q(v)-A\bigr).
\]
Consequently, for every $w\in\operatorname{supp}\mu_{\varepsilon,v}$ with $\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon$-null $v$ as above,
\[
    -\varepsilon\bigl(Q(v)+A+B\bigr)
    =L_\varepsilon(v) -\varepsilon(A+B)
    \leq L(w)
    \leq-\varepsilon\bigl(Q(v)-A-B\bigr)
    \leq-\varepsilon.
\]
Since $Q$ is bounded on $\mathcal K$, it follows that
\[
    \operatorname{supp}\mu_{\varepsilon,v}
    \subseteq
    \{|L|\leq c\varepsilon\}\cap\Omega^C\cap \mathcal K'
\]
for some $c>0$, proving the claim.

By the distributional null energy condition, the uniform boundedness of $a_{\varepsilon,v}$ together with the first part of the proof implies
\[
    \liminf_{\varepsilon\to0}
    \inf_{v\in \mathcal K\cap\Omega^C[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] \cap \widecheck L_\varepsilon^{-1}(0)}
    \mathcal H_\varepsilon\mathrm{Ric}[L](v)\geq0.
\]
Together with the locally uniform convergence $\mathrm{Ric}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] -\mathcal H_\varepsilon\mathrm{Ric}[L]\rightarrow0$ established in the proof of Proposition \ref{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound}, we obtain the claimed estimate.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{Prop: approx weighted energy conditions}
Let $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ be a $C^{(1,2)}$ Finsler spacetime $(M,L)$ equipped with a $C^1$ measure $\mathfrak m$, and let $N \in (-\infty,1] \cup (n,\infty]$ in \textit{(i)} and $N \in (-\infty,2] \cup (n,\infty]$ with $n \ge 3$ in \textit{(ii)}.
\begin{enumerate}
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}\label{appRN1}
    If $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ satisfies $\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N \ge K$ timelike distributionally for $K \in \mathbb R$, then for every compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq \Omega^T$ and $\delta > 0$, there exists $\varepsilon_0 = \varepsilon_0(\mathcal K,\delta) > 0$ such that for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0)$ and $v \in \mathcal K$ with $2\widecheck L_\varepsilon(v) = -1$, we have
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:WeightedGeneralRicciApprox}
        \mathrm{Ric}^N_{\mathfrak m_\varepsilon}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon](v) \geq K - \delta.
    \end{equation}
    \refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}}\label{appRN2}
    If $(M,L,\mathfrak m)$ satisfies the distributional null $N$-energy condition, then for every compact set $\mathcal K \subseteq TM \setminus \mathbf 0$ and $\delta > 0$, there exists $\varepsilon_0 = \varepsilon_0(\mathcal K,\delta) > 0$ such that for all $\varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0)$ and $v \in \mathcal K \cap \Omega^C[\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon]$ with $\widecheck L_\varepsilon(v) = 0$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{Ric}^N_{\mathfrak m_\varepsilon}[\widecheck L_\varepsilon](v) \geq -\delta.
    \end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
For simplicity of the argument, we assume $M = \mathbb R^n$ and $\mathfrak m_\varepsilon = \mathfrak m \star \rho^\mathsf x_\varepsilon$. The general case follows easily from the arguments we give here.
\begin{enumerate}
\refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} As in the proof of Proposition \ref{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound}, we show that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ProofWeightedRicci}
    \| \mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m_\varepsilon}^N[\widecheck L_\varepsilon] - \mathcal H_\varepsilon(\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N[L]) \|_{\infty, \mathcal{K}} \rightarrow 0
\end{equation}
on any compact $\mathcal K\subseteq TM\setminus\mathbf 0$; cf.\ also the proof of Corollary \ref{cor:GeneralRicciBound}. By $2$-homogeneity, it suffices to restrict our attention to $v \in \mathcal K$ having unit $\tilde g$-norm. 

To this end, we first compare $\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m_\varepsilon}^N[L_\varepsilon] - (\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N[L])\star \rho_\varepsilon$ term by term using the formula 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N[L] =\frac{\partial G^i}{\partial x^i} - N^i_kN^k_i- v^iv^j\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^i\partial x^j} + G^i\frac{\partial u}{\partial x^i} -\frac{1}{N-n}\left(N^i_i-v^j\frac{\partial u}{\partial x^j}\right)^2,
\end{equation*}
where $u=\log \sigma$, established in Remark \ref{rmk:WeightedRicciSimplified}.

The first term can be handled exactly as in Proposition \ref{Prop: ApproxtimelikeRiccibound}, using $G^i = g^{il} A_l$ and the fact that $g^{il} \in C^{(1,0)}$ and $A_l \in C^{(0,1)}$. 
Moreover, $C^{(1,2)}_\mathrm{loc}$-convergence of $L_\varepsilon$ to $L$ gives
\begin{equation*}
    G^i_\varepsilon \rightarrow G^i, \qquad (N_\varepsilon)^i_j \rightarrow N^i_j
\end{equation*}
locally uniformly.
Thus, using $u_\varepsilon := \log \sigma_\varepsilon \rightarrow u = \log \sigma$ in $C^1_\mathrm{loc}$, we see that the second, fourth, and fifth terms in the difference converge to $0$ locally uniformly.

Finally, setting
\begin{equation*}
    a_\varepsilon:= \sigma_\varepsilon^{-1}, \qquad a:= \sigma^{-1}, \qquad f_j:= \frac{\partial \sigma}{\partial x^j},
\end{equation*}
we have 
\begin{equation*}
    a_\varepsilon \rightarrow a \quad \textnormal{in}\ C^{1}_\mathrm{loc}, \qquad \|a_\varepsilon - a\|_{\infty, \mathcal K} = O(\varepsilon)
\end{equation*}
and we can write
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{\partial u_\varepsilon}{\partial x^j} = a_\varepsilon (f_j \star \rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf x}), \qquad \frac{\partial u}{\partial x^j} = a f_j.
\end{equation*}
Hence, applying the Friedrichs estimate \eqref{eq:Friedrichs}, we get
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{\partial^2 u_\varepsilon}{\partial x^i \partial x^j} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x^i}\left( \frac{\partial u}{\partial x^j} \star \rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf x}\right) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x^i}\bigl(a_\varepsilon (f_j \star \rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf x})\bigr) - \frac{\partial}{\partial x^i}\bigl((af_j) \star \rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf x}\bigr) \rightarrow 0
\end{equation*}
locally uniformly.
Now, convolving with respect to $\rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf v}$ and reintroducing the coefficients $v^iv^j$ locally produces an error term of the form
\begin{equation*}
    \left(v^iv^j-\int(v^i-w^i)(v^j-w^j) \rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf v}(w) \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d} w \right)\partial_{x^i}(f_j \star \rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf x}).
\end{equation*}
As $|w|$ is $O(\varepsilon)$ on the support of $\rho_\varepsilon^{\mathsf v}$ and $f_j$ is continuous, we can appeal to \eqref{eq:ConvergenceC0derivative} to conclude $v^iv^j \partial_{x^i}\partial_{x^j} u_\varepsilon - (v^iv^j \partial_{x^i}\partial_{x^j} u)\star \rho_\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$ locally uniformly, proving that $\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m_\varepsilon}^N[L_\varepsilon] - (\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m}^N[L])\star \rho_\varepsilon$ converges to $0$.

To finally get \eqref{eq:ProofWeightedRicci}, we use \eqref{eq:WeightedRicciSimplified} to see $\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m_\varepsilon}^N[\widecheck{L}_\varepsilon] -\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak m_\varepsilon}^N[L_\varepsilon] \to 0$.
The Hessian terms cancel (since $u_\varepsilon$ is common), while $\partial_{x^i}(\widecheck G_\varepsilon^i-G_\varepsilon^i)$
tends to $0$ locally uniformly by \eqref{eq:GNN}.
The difference of the terms $N^i_kN^k_i$ tends to $0$ locally uniformly by \eqref{eq:EstimatesCheckSpray} and the local uniform boundedness of the connection coefficients.
It therefore suffices to argue that both
\begin{equation*}
    (\widecheck G_\varepsilon^i - G_\varepsilon^i) \frac{\partial u_\varepsilon}{\partial x^i}  \quad \textnormal{and} \quad \left((\widecheck N_\varepsilon)^i_i - v^j\frac{\partial u_\varepsilon}{\partial x^j}\right)^2- \left((N_\varepsilon)^i_i -v^j\frac{\partial u_\varepsilon}{\partial x^j}\right)^2
\end{equation*}
converge to $0$ locally uniformly, where the last term is omitted in the case $N = \infty$. 
This, in turn, follows easily from the local uniform boundedness of $\partial_{x^i} u_\varepsilon$ and \eqref{eq:EstimatesCheckSpray}. 
From \eqref{eq:ProofWeightedRicci}, we simply repeat the argument given in the proof of Corollary \ref{cor:GeneralRicciBound} to get \eqref{eq:WeightedGeneralRicciApprox}.

\refstepcounter{inlineenum}{(\roman{inlineenum})\hspace{\thelabelsep}} By \eqref{eq:ProofWeightedRicci}, this is entirely analogous to the proof of Proposition \ref{Prop: Null approximate Ricci}. \qedhere
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- definition-13: body_md -->
We say that $(M,L)$ is \emph{non-totally imprisoning} if no inextendible future causal curve is contained in a compact set.
<!-- end definition-13 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
If $(M,L)$ is non-totally imprisoning, then for every compact set $K \subseteq M$, there is a constant $C > 0$ that bounds the $\tilde g$-lengths of all future causal curves contained in $K$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- definition-14: body_md -->
We say that $(M,L)$ is \emph{globally hyperbolic} if it is non-totally imprisoning and the causal diamond $J(x,y) := J^+(x) \cap J^-(y)$ is compact for every $x,y \in M$.
<!-- end definition-14 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
Let $(M,L)$ be a globally hyperbolic $C^{(0,2)}$ Finsler spacetime. 
    Then there exists a smooth time-oriented Lorentz--Finsler structure $L'$ on $M$ such that $L \triangleleft L'$ and $(M,L')$ is non-totally imprisoning.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We can follow the lines of the proof of \cite[Thm.\ 4.3]{Saemann}. We sketch the most important points. 
    Let $\pi:TM \to M$ be the canonical projection.
    
    First, we show that for every relatively compact open set $B \subseteq M$, there exists a $C^{(0,2)}$ Lorentz--Finsler structure $\widetilde{L}$ such that $\widetilde{L} \triangleright L$ on $B$, $\widetilde{L} = L$ on $M \setminus B$, and that $(M,\widetilde{L})$ is non-totally imprisoning.
    To this end, fix an arbitrary smooth function $\beta_B$ on $M$ such that $\beta_B >0$ on $B$ and $\beta_B=0$ on $M \setminus B$, and set for $s>0$ and $v \in TM$
    \[
    L_s(v) :=L(v) -\frac{s}{2}\beta_B\bigl( \pi(v) \bigr) \tilde{g}(v,v).
    \]
    Since $L$ is $C^{(0,2)}$ and $B$ is relatively compact, for some $s'>0$ and any $s \in (0,s')$, $L_s$ is a Lorentz--Finsler structure time-oriented by the same vector field as $L$.
    By construction, clearly $L \triangleleft L_s$ on $B$ and $L=L_s$ on $M \setminus B$.
    Now, we can show that there exists $s'' \in (0,s')$ such that $L_s$ is non-totally imprisoning for every $s \in (0,s'')$.
    This is argued by contradiction. 
    Suppose that there is a sequence $(s_m)_{m \in \mathbb N}$ such that $s_m \to 0$ and $(M,L_{s_m})$ is totally imprisoning. 
    As in \cite[Thm.\ 4.3]{Saemann}, this leads to a contradiction to the global hyperbolicity of $(M,L)$. 
    Thus, choosing $\widetilde{L}=L_s$ for small $s>0$ shows the claim.
